Health Advisor build — session review

Claude Code session · 10 October 2026 · 1:02 PM – 9:21 PM · generated from session-log.txt · live at health-advisor-eta.vercel.app

Summary

Over 39 steps, the session went from an empty folder to a deployed, tested diabetes-assessment app: two planning blueprints, a phase plan, 40+ specification files, a rules engine, an API, a LangGraph AI pipeline that passed a live evaluation, a Next.js frontend, PDF and share links, submission evidence, and a production deployment on Vercel + Google Cloud Run. All ten build phases are done.

Key outcomes

  • Live: https://health-advisor-eta.vercel.app (Vercel, Mumbai) → API on Google Cloud Run (Mumbai) → Neon PostgreSQL (Singapore), traced in Phoenix Cloud. Expected cost ≈ $0/month at demo traffic plus Claude usage per report.
  • Scope reset: the first broad health-content blueprint (about 20 months of work) was set aside for a 2-day diabetes assessment build.
  • Specification-driven: specs, JSON schemas and clinical rule YAMLs came first; code reads or is generated from them; the Gherkin acceptance criteria run as tests (33 scenarios, mutation-checked).
  • Live AI evaluation (16 golden cases): 0 unsafe reports shown, 100% delivered without fallback, 94% passed safety first time, p95 latency 25.2 s.
  • Hosting decision: AWS (≈ $35/month) was replaced by Cloud Run + Neon + Phoenix Cloud (ADR 0005) after comparing cheaper options.
  • Problems found and fixed along the way: safety-check false positives, fallback-text YAML bugs, risk indicators listing too much, a hydration race, a cold-start PDF delay, a Docker image that would have shipped the .env file, a Cloud Build permission gap, traces lost on Cloud Run, and a font-cache issue.

    Step 6: I am fine with the blue print. Lets plan the build in phases before we start implementing. Generate a phase wise appr…

    done 3:13 PM36s of work

    Your prompt

    I am fine with the blue print. Lets plan the build in phases before we start implementing. Generate a phase wise approach for this build

    Outcome

    Here's how I'd split the 2 days into phases. Each phase ends with a check you can run before the next one starts. The AWS skeleton goes live at the end of day 1, so any deployment surprises show up while there's still time to fix them. Day 1: specs, engine, AI (about 8.5 h)
    Full reply (16 paragraphs)
    Here's how I'd split the 2 days into phases. Each phase ends with a check you can run before the next one starts. The AWS skeleton goes live at the end of day 1, so any deployment surprises show up while there's still time to fix them. Day 1: specs, engine, AI (about 8.5 h) Phase 0: Setup (30 min) - Create the repo with the folder layout from the blueprint, run git init, and add a pre-commit secret scan. - Backend: Python 3.12 with uv, FastAPI and LangGraph. Frontend: Next.js with TypeScript and pnpm. - A local docker-compose file runs Postgres 16 and Phoenix. - In parallel, start creating the RDS database on AWS, since that takes 10–15 minutes. - Check: docker compose up gives a working Postgres and the Phoenix UI on :6006. Phase 1: Core specs (2.5 h) - Write the 12 core specs. Start with the ones code will read directly: a. The three JSON schemas: input, state, report. b. The clinical rule files: classification-rules.md, monitoring-rules.yaml, comorbidity-constraints.yaml, references.md. c. banned-terms.yaml and the guardrails spec. d. The LangGraph spec and the 3 prompt files. e. openapi.yaml and the data model. f. Vision, PRD, user journeys, F1–F5, the Gherkin acceptance tests, the evaluation spec with 20 golden cases, and the test strategy. - Check: every spec has front-matter, the schemas validate, the golden cases file holds 20 cases with their expected rule outputs, and the monitoring rules have been checked against the ADA guidelines. Phase 2: Rules engine (1.5 h) - Write classify, metrics (BMI with Asian cut-offs, calorie target), risk and monitoring. They read from the YAML files rather than hard-coding values. - Add pytest tests for the boundary values (5.6, 5.7, 6.4, 6.5, 10.0, 2.9, 20.1) and the eligibility exits. - Check: all rule tests pass, and all 20 golden cases match on rule outputs. Phase 3: API and database (1 h) - Add Alembic migrations for the 4 tables and Pydantic models generated from the schemas. - Build POST /classify and POST /assessments, with the LLM nodes stubbed out for now, plus GET /reports/{id} and /health. - Check: contract tests pass against openapi.yaml, and an assessment is saved with a report built only from the rules. Phase 4: LangGraph and the LLM (2.5 h) - Build the 11-node graph: the diet and lifestyle writers run in parallel, the safety check retries once, and the rule-only fallback catches a second failure. - LLM output comes back as structured JSON through Claude Sonnet 5 tool use, checked with Pydantic. - Turn on Phoenix tracing through OpenInference. - Check: all 20 golden cases run end to end, the safety check passes at least 95% of them, an ineligible case shows zero LLM calls in Phoenix, and an assessment finishes in under 30 s at p95. Phase 5: Deploy a basic version to AWS (30 min) - EC2 with Docker Compose (API, Phoenix and Caddy), connected to RDS, with secrets in SSM. Connect Amplify with a placeholder Next.js page. - Check: the public https://…/api/health URL returns 200. Day 2: product, output, ship (about 7 h) Phase 6: Frontend (3.5 h) - Landing page with the disclaimer, then the 3-step form, validated against the generated TypeScript types. - The classification appears right after step 1, followed by a progress screen and the 10-section report page with the urgent banner. - Basic accessibility: labels, keyboard navigation, contrast. - Check: the Meena, Priya (pregnant) and Joseph (urgent) journeys all work in the browser. Phase 7: PDF, share link and feedback (1 h) - A WeasyPrint template reuses the report layout. Share links can be created, viewed and revoked, and expire after 30 days. Add the feedback endpoint. - Check: the PDF downloads in under 5 s, and a revoked link returns 404. Phase 8: Production deploy and hardening (1.5 h) - Deploy the full app. Add rate limiting, restrict CORS to the Amplify domain, put basic auth on Phoenix, lock down security groups and add the 90-day retention job. - Check: a smoke test passes on the live URL, and Phoenix shows traces from production. Phase 9: Evidence and submission (1 h) - Run the full acceptance suite, fill in the traceability matrix, write the README with the demo link, take Phoenix screenshots and record the 5-minute demo. - Check: every acceptance criterion in Section 11 of the blueprint is ticked. If you fall behind, cut in this order: feedback, then share-link revocation, then the LLM-judge part of the safety check (the rule-based checks stay), then extra regional cuisines. As a last resort, run Next.js on the same EC2 instead of Amplify. Before Phase 0, I need three things from you: 1. An Anthropic API key, kept in a local .env file. Don't paste it here. 2. AWS account access and a region (I'd suggest ap-south-1 if you're in India). 3. Whether health-advisor/ should be the repo root. Once you confirm, I'll save this plan as specs/00-build-plan.md and start Phase 0.
  7. 7

    Step 7: Can we use Vercel for frontend deployment

    done 3:15 PM14s of work

    Your prompt

    Can we use Vercel for frontend deployment

    Outcome

    Yes, Vercel works for the frontend, and it's probably the quicker choice for a 2-day build. The backend (FastAPI, LangGraph, Phoenix and RDS) stays on AWS, so you still meet the AWS requirement. Why it helps - Vercel builds Next.js automatically, so there's nothing to configure. - Every push gets its own preview URL, which is handy for the demo. - It saves about 20–30 minutes compared with setting up Amplify.
    Full reply (5 paragraphs)
    Yes, Vercel works for the frontend, and it's probably the quicker choice for a 2-day build. The backend (FastAPI, LangGraph, Phoenix and RDS) stays on AWS, so you still meet the AWS requirement. Why it helps - Vercel builds Next.js automatically, so there's nothing to configure. - Every push gets its own preview URL, which is handy for the demo. - It saves about 20–30 minutes compared with setting up Amplify. What changes - Check your brief first. If it says the whole system must run on AWS, keep Amplify. If it only says the deployment uses AWS, frontend on Vercel and backend on AWS is a normal split you can justify. - The API needs real HTTPS. A Vercel page served over HTTPS can't call a plain-HTTP API, because browsers block that. Caddy can get a certificate automatically, but only for a domain name, not a bare IP address. Use a Route 53 subdomain, or a free name like api.<elastic-ip>.sslip.io. - Calling the API: I'd use a Next.js rewrite so /api/* on Vercel is forwarded to the EC2 API. The browser then only talks to the Vercel domain, so you skip CORS setup entirely. Share links also stay on one domain. - Data path: with the rewrite, health inputs pass through Vercel, which is US-hosted by default, on their way to AWS. That's fine for an academic project, and you can name it as a data-residency point for V2. - Secrets: the frontend needs only the API base URL, stored as a Vercel environment variable. The Anthropic key and database URL stay in AWS SSM. Changes to the build plan - Phase 0: connect the GitHub repo to Vercel, with frontend/ as the root directory. - Phase 5: deploy to Vercel instead of Amplify, and set up the API domain plus the rewrite. - Phase 8: drop the CORS hardening because the rewrite makes it unnecessary. Add a check that vercel.app preview URLs can't reach the production API, or point preview builds at a separate staging API. I'll update the blueprint document to match: the at-a-glance table, the Section 5 diagram and the Section 8 deployment table. Want me to go ahead, along with your answers to the three Phase 0 questions?

    • 255a0ce feat(AI-001): phase 4 LangGraph assessment graph

    Outcome

    Phase 4, the LangGraph graph and the AI, is built and committed as 255a0ce. The live run against Claude is still pending, because it needs your API key. How a full assessment runs: 1. Five rule steps run first: eligibility, classification, BMI and calories, risk factors, and monitoring. 2. The diet and lifestyle writers run in parallel. The summary writer runs after both finish. 3. Safety check S3 rejects any meat, fish, egg or dairy that breaks the user's diet preference, then the other rule checks run. If all pass, the LLM safety judge reviews the content. 4. On a failure, the writers retry once, and they receive the list of problems found. 5. If the retry also fails, if Claude is down, or if the judge can't respond, the report uses the safe standard text and is still complete. - Without an API key the graph skips straight to the standard text, so local development keeps working.
    Full reply (9 paragraphs)
    Phase 4, the LangGraph graph and the AI, is built and committed as 255a0ce. The live run against Claude is still pending, because it needs your API key. How a full assessment runs: 1. Five rule steps run first: eligibility, classification, BMI and calories, risk factors, and monitoring. 2. The diet and lifestyle writers run in parallel. The summary writer runs after both finish. 3. Safety check S3 rejects any meat, fish, egg or dairy that breaks the user's diet preference, then the other rule checks run. If all pass, the LLM safety judge reviews the content. 4. On a failure, the writers retry once, and they receive the list of problems found. 5. If the retry also fails, if Claude is down, or if the judge can't respond, the report uses the safe standard text and is still complete. - Without an API key the graph skips straight to the standard text, so local development keeps working. One change from the specs, with the reason: the nodes call Claude through the official anthropic SDK, with structured output checked against the Pydantic models. LangChain's wrapper isn't used. Claude Sonnet 5 rejects the temperature setting the specs had planned, so I removed it. ADR 0003 and AI-001 are updated to match. Model choice: the blueprint you approved uses Claude Sonnet 5. The Claude API guidance defaults to the larger Claude Opus 5, which costs about 2.5× more per token. Sonnet 5 is a reasonable fit for these writing tasks, but you can switch with LLM_MODEL=claude-opus-5 in .env. Tests: 196 pass with 96% coverage. A fake LLM covers the happy path, writers running in parallel, retry with feedback, fallback after two failures, and an outage. It also shows that ineligible users trigger zero LLM calls, and that the judge fails closed when it can't respond. Phoenix check: I ran two assessments with tracing on. Each showed up as one trace with its graph nodes as child spans, and the referral had no LLM spans. Bugs fixed along the way: - HbA1c check vs. urgent message: the number check rejected the rule-written urgent message "Your HbA1c is 10% or higher". Unfixed, every urgent user would have received only standard text. - Judge prompt YAML: the judge prompt's header would have crashed the graph when it started. What I need from you next: 1. API key: add ANTHROPIC_API_KEY= to backend/.env when it's ready. Then start Phoenix and run the live evaluation: cd backend && uv run phoenix serve cd backend && uv run python -m evals.run_golden It costs real money: about 4 model calls per case, 68 calls for the full set. It writes a results file into specs/quality/eval-results/. 2. Phase 5 (skeleton deploy to AWS and Vercel): your AWS CLI's default region is us-east-1, and I couldn't confirm it's logged in. Please run ! aws sts get-caller-identity and ! vercel whoami so I can see which accounts we're deploying to. Phase 5 creates paid AWS resources, so I'll show you exactly what it will create before running anything. The resources are an EC2 instance, an RDS database, an Elastic IP and SSM parameters, at roughly $25–35 a month.

    Step 14: give me the steps to create Anthropic API key

    done 4:22 PM17s of work

    Your prompt

    give me the steps to create Anthropic API key

    Outcome

    Here's how to create the key and connect it to the app: 1. Sign in to the Claude Console at https://platform.claude.com. If you don't have an account, sign up with your email and verify it. 2. Add credits. Go to Settings → Billing and buy prepaid credits; $5–10 is plenty for this project. API keys don't work on an account with no credit. 3. Set a spend limit (recommended). Under Settings → Limits, set a low monthly limit, such as $10, so a bug or a leaked key can't run up a large bill. 4. Create the key. Go to Settings → API Keys, click Create Key and give it a name like health-advisor-dev. 5. Copy it immediately. It starts with sk-ant-… and is shown only once. If you lose it, delete it and create a new one. 6. Add it to the project. Open backend/.env in your editor and set: ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=sk-ant-...your key... Git already ignores .env. Don't paste the key into this chat, a commit or the frontend. 7. Tell me when it's in. I'll do a one-case test run first, then the full evaluation.
    Full reply (4 paragraphs)
    Here's how to create the key and connect it to the app: 1. Sign in to the Claude Console at https://platform.claude.com. If you don't have an account, sign up with your email and verify it. 2. Add credits. Go to Settings → Billing and buy prepaid credits; $5–10 is plenty for this project. API keys don't work on an account with no credit. 3. Set a spend limit (recommended). Under Settings → Limits, set a low monthly limit, such as $10, so a bug or a leaked key can't run up a large bill. 4. Create the key. Go to Settings → API Keys, click Create Key and give it a name like health-advisor-dev. 5. Copy it immediately. It starts with sk-ant-… and is shown only once. If you lose it, delete it and create a new one. 6. Add it to the project. Open backend/.env in your editor and set: ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=sk-ant-...your key... Git already ignores .env. Don't paste the key into this chat, a commit or the frontend. 7. Tell me when it's in. I'll do a one-case test run first, then the full evaluation. Rough cost: each assessment makes about 4 calls to Claude Sonnet 5, at $2 per million input tokens and $10 per million output tokens. That's somewhere around $0.05–0.15 per assessment, so the full 17-case evaluation should be about $1–3. This is an estimate. The Console's Usage page shows the actual spend after the first run. For the AWS deployment later: the key will go into AWS Parameter Store as an encrypted secret, never into the Docker image or the repo. You may want a separate health-advisor-prod key for that, so you can revoke the two independently.

Long replies show the first part, with "Full reply" to expand. The terminal's box-drawn tables (such as the eval results) are turned into real tables. "Expand all" and "Collapse all" buttons and a "Jump to a step" index are included. The page adapts to dark mode and phone widths.  Created frontend/lib/focus-error.ts (+10 -0)
       1 +/** NFR-4: after a failed submit, move focus to the first field
         + with an error so keyboard and
       2 + * screen-reader users land on it. `keys` are element ids or ra
         +dio/checkbox group names, in form order. */
       3 +export function focusFirstError(keys: string[], errors: Record<
         +string, string | undefined>) {
       4 +  const first = keys.find((k) => errors[k]);
       5 +  if (!first) return;
       6 +  requestAnimationFrame(() => {
       7 +    const el = document.getElementById(first) ?? document.query
         +Selector<HTMLElement>(`[name="${first}"]`);
       8 +    el?.focus();
       9 +  });
      10 +}

    Step 32: any other alternate to host backend code other than AWS

    Your prompt

    any other alternate to host backend code other than AWS

    Outcome

    Yes, there are several good non-AWS options. Many are simpler and cheaper for a project this size. One thing to check first: your original brief listed "Deployment – AWS" in the tech stack. If your course requires AWS, stay on AWS with the cheaper Lightsail option (about $7–12 a month). If it doesn't, these are the realistic alternatives: Option: Google Cloud Run + Neon + Phoenix Cloud What runs where: API container on Cloud Run (Mumbai, asia-south1); PostgreSQL on Neon's free tier; traces on Arize's hosted Phoenix (free tier) Est. cost / month: ≈ $0 within free tiers Pros: Scales to zero, so you pay nothing when idle; HTTPS built in; uses our existing Dockerfile Cons: Cold start of a few seconds after idle; three accounts to set up ──────────────────────────────────────── Option: Render What runs where: API as a Docker web service; Render PostgreSQL Est. cost / month: ≈ $7 (API) + ≈ $6–7 (DB) ≈ $13–14 Pros: Very simple, deploys from GitHub, HTTPS included Cons: The free tier sleeps after 15 minutes, which is too slow for a demo; the
    Full reply (7 paragraphs)
    Yes, there are several good non-AWS options. Many are simpler and cheaper for a project this size. One thing to check first: your original brief listed "Deployment – AWS" in the tech stack. If your course requires AWS, stay on AWS with the cheaper Lightsail option (about $7–12 a month). If it doesn't, these are the realistic alternatives: Option: Google Cloud Run + Neon + Phoenix Cloud What runs where: API container on Cloud Run (Mumbai, asia-south1); PostgreSQL on Neon's free tier; traces on Arize's hosted Phoenix (free tier) Est. cost / month: ≈ $0 within free tiers Pros: Scales to zero, so you pay nothing when idle; HTTPS built in; uses our existing Dockerfile Cons: Cold start of a few seconds after idle; three accounts to set up ──────────────────────────────────────── Option: Render What runs where: API as a Docker web service; Render PostgreSQL Est. cost / month: ≈ $7 (API) + ≈ $6–7 (DB) ≈ $13–14 Pros: Very simple, deploys from GitHub, HTTPS included Cons: The free tier sleeps after 15 minutes, which is too slow for a demo; the free DB expires after 30 days ──────────────────────────────────────── Option: Railway What runs where: API (Docker) + PostgreSQL in one project Est. cost / month: ≈ $5–10 (usage-based; the $5 Hobby plan includes $5 of usage) Pros: Easiest setup; DB and app side by side Cons: Usage-based billing is less predictable ──────────────────────────────────────── Option: Fly.io What runs where: API container + Neon/Supabase for the DB Est. cost / month: ≈ $3–6 (+ a free DB) Pros: Cheap, has an Indian region (Chennai) Cons: Needs a card, CLI-driven setup ──────────────────────────────────────── Option: VPS (Hetzner / DigitalOcean) What runs where: One server running our Docker Compose (API, Postgres, Phoenix, Caddy) Est. cost / month: Hetzner ≈ €4–5 · DigitalOcean ≈ $12 (2 GB, Bangalore) Pros: Cheapest flat price; our deploy/ files work almost as-is Cons: You manage the server (updates, backups) ──────────────────────────────────────── Option: Oracle Cloud Always Free What runs where: Same single-server layout on a free ARM VM Est. cost / month: $0 Pros: Genuinely free indefinitely Cons: Sign-up and capacity can be difficult These prices are from my general knowledge, so check each provider's pricing page before deciding. My recommendation: - Cheapest: Google Cloud Run + Neon + Phoenix Cloud, effectively free at demo traffic. It needs small changes: no Caddy (Cloud Run provides HTTPS), migrations run as a one-off job, and Phoenix gets an API key setting. The Vercel /api rewrite works unchanged. - Simplest: Railway, which is one dashboard and a few dollars a month. Whichever you choose, I'll record the decision as ADR 0005 and update the deployment spec and blueprint, so the change of host is documented rather than unexplained. Which way do you want to go: Cloud Run, Railway, a VPS, or Lightsail if AWS is required? Also, is AWS a course requirement?

    Full reply (10 paragraphs)
    The app is live: - App: https://health-advisor-eta.vercel.app - API: https://health-advisor-api-1099158459764.asia-south1.run.app on Google Cloud Run (Mumbai), with the database on Neon (Singapore) and traces in Phoenix Cloud. Production smoke test:
    CheckResult
    Home page and the /api rewrite to Cloud RunWorking
    Full AI report (Meena; Joseph, urgent)26 s, Claude Sonnet 5, passed the safety check first time
    PDF download1.1–1.6 s once warm; up to 4.5 s just after a fresh deploy
    Share link and the expired-link pageWorking
    Referral (pregnant user)0.4 s, no AI call
    Phoenix Cloud tracesA full assessment trace with all 4 AI calls
    Problems found in production and fixed: 1. Build permission: Cloud Build couldn't read the uploaded source, because new Google Cloud projects don't give the build account permissions. The deploy script now grants the narrow "builder" role. 2. Missing traces: assessment traces never reached Phoenix, because Cloud Run throttles the CPU after a response is sent. Traces are now flushed before each assessment returns. 3. Font cache: PDFs rebuilt their font cache on every request. The cache is now built into the image, and the errors are gone. Security notes: - Before the first deploy, I found the Dockerfile would have copied your .env file, with the API key, into the image. Ignore files now keep .env files out, and I checked the exact upload list (no .env) before deploying. - The secrets live in Google Secret Manager, and the production API key isn't in the repo. - Earlier I printed a Phoenix key by mistake. You replaced it, and I confirmed the new key differs from the exposed one. Cost: about $0/month at demo traffic, since everything runs within free tiers. The exceptions are Claude usage of roughly $0.05–0.15 per report, and the default compute service account keeping the builder role, which costs nothing. The site is public, so I'd still set a monthly spend limit in the Anthropic Console. Behaviour to expect: the first report after a quiet period takes a few extra seconds, because both Cloud Run and Neon start from zero. Records: ADR 0005 and the deployment spec record the move from AWS to Google Cloud. The build plan marks all phases 0–9 done, the README links the live demo, and the acceptance evidence includes the production results. All 271 backend tests pass, and everything is committed (latest 43de492 plus the deployed 418c6cd). Left for you: - Record the demo video, using specs/quality/demo-script.md; you can demo on the live site now. - Check the PDF layout by eye with Download PDF on the live site. - Optionally, do a VoiceOver pass before any wider launch. - After grading, run PROJECT_ID=health-advisor-511214 ./deploy/teardown-gcp.sh to remove the Google resources. It asks you to type the project ID first, and lists what to delete manually in Vercel, Neon, Phoenix and Anthropic.
